Enable Automation of AIDE Database Check\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h5\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eTo move from manual oversight to automated alerting, you should establish a recurring scheduled task. This ensures that the VCSA programmatically verifies its own state and reports any discrepancies.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eOpen crontab:\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"]]],[null,null,[null,[null,"\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode\u003ecrontab -e\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e"]]],[null,null,[null,[null,"\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eAdd the following edit to configure the task:\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"]]],[null,null,[null,[null,"\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode\u003e# Execute check every 6 hours and send results via VCSA remote syslog\r\n0 */6 * * * /usr/bin/aide --check | logger -t AIDE_TRAP -p local6.crit\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e"]]],[null,null,[null,[null,"\u003ch5\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e8. Conduct a Test Event\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h5\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eTo confirm your defense is operational and your SIEM is successfully receiving AIDE alerts, perform a simulated breach.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eAdd a comment to a monitored area (e.g., /etc/rc.local):\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"]]],[null,null,[null,[null,"\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode\u003eecho \u0026quot;# Forensic Bridge Test\u0026quot; \u0026gt;\u0026gt; /etc/rc.local\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e"]]],[null,null,[null,[null,"\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eTrigger a remote event trap:\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"]]],[null,null,[null,[null,"\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode\u003eaide --check | logger -t AIDE_TRAP -p local6.crit\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e"]]],[null,null,[null,[null,"\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eVerify the Alert: Check the VCSA remote syslog target for the tag AIDE_TRAP:\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"]]],[null,null,[null,[null,"\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode\u003eAIDE found differences between database and filesystem!! followed by Changed files: /etc/rc.local.\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e"]]],[null,null,[null,[null,"\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eVCSA Shell History\u003c/span\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e \u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eOn a Photon-based VCSA, the \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e/root/.bash_history\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e file is not replicated to any other log file, nor is it sent to a remote syslog by default. This represents a major forensic visibility gap that threat actors take advantage of to maintain their unmonitored persistence.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eThe Buffer Issue:\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e Commands typed into the shell are kept in a memory buffer. They are only written (appended) to the physical file on the disk when the user logs out of the session.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eThe Anti-Forensics Risk:\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e If a threat actor gains shell access, their first move is often to run \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eunset HISTFILE\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e or \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003ehistory -c\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e. This prevents the memory buffer from ever being written to the disk. Even if the file is written, an attacker can simply run \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003erm /root/.bash_history\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e before exiting.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eNo Remote Transmission:\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e Standard VCSA syslog configurations monitor directories like \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e/var/log/\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e. They do not monitor hidden user files like \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e.bash_history\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eThe reason the auditd remote syslog discussed in the \u003c/span\u003e\u003ca href\u003d\"https://docs.google.com/document/d/1Qdj2nlx3yV1KoNveQ5lxFFyslKdhNXLEccIHHuYqEc8/edit?tab\u003dt.0#heading\u003dh.q04njdd8vhz4\" rel\u003d\"noopener\" target\u003d\"_blank\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eprevious steps\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e is so critical is that it bypasses the need for \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e.bash_history\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e entirely. auditd intercepts system calls (syscalls) at the kernel level and exfiltrates detailed forensic data including the original User ID (AUID) and command outcomes to a remote SIEM as the command is executed. This bridge ensures that even if a threat actor purges local logs or crashes the session, an immutable, real-time audit trail remains securely preserved off-appliance.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eLogging Design Principles\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eRecent \u003c/span\u003e\u003ca href\u003d\"https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/brickstorm-espionage-campaign?e\u003d48754805\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eCISA reporting and GTIG analysis \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003edescribe threat actors abusing management interfaces (including enabling SSH), making persistence-related configuration changes, and using vCenter capabilities to access high-value virtual machines. An organization's logging strategy should therefore prioritize management-plane audit trails, service-state changes, identity events, hypervisor telemetry, and centralized forwarding.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003col\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:decimal;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eCentralize first, then tune.\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e Forward logs off-host in near real time so an attacker cannot tamper with them by wiping local disks. Configure both VCSA and ESXi to forward to a central syslog/SIEM target.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:decimal;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eTreat logs as Tier-0 data.\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e If vCenter is Tier-0, then vCenter/ESXi logs are also Tier-0. Restrict who can read them, who can change forwarding settings, and who can stop logging services.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:decimal;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eMake timestamps defensible. \u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eEnsure consistent Network Time Protocol (NTP) across VCSA, ESXi hosts, jump boxes, and log collectors so correlation is reliable during an incident.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:decimal;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eLog the actions that matter, not everything. \u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eFor threat actor activity, you care less about generic \"system is running\" noise and more about: who accessed management, what changed, what was cloned/exported, what services were enabled, what binaries/configs were modified, and where the appliance/host talked to on the network.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ol\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eOrganizations should establish a \"\u003c/span\u003e\u003ca href\u003d\"https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/defending-vsphere-from-unc3944?e\u003d48754805\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003evSphere logging fundamentals\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\" previously described by Mandiant by offloading all infrastructure logs to a centralized, remote SIEM. \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch5\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eThe vSphere Unified Logging Architecture\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h5\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eThe following summary table provides a definitive map of the vSphere telemetry streams described. The following standards are required to ensure the integrity and survivability of the forensic trail:\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eEncryption via TLS (TCP Port 6514):\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e Sending logs over UDP/514 is insecure and unreliable. Threat actors can access management traffic or spoof log entries. Organizations should enforce TCP with TLS encryption for all syslog traffic. This ensures that logs are encrypted in transit and guarantees delivery through the TCP handshake.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eCertificate Validation:\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e To prevent man-in-the-middle (MitM) attacks on the logging pipeline, the VCSA and ESXi hosts should be configured to validate the SSL certificate of the remote syslog server. This ensures that telemetry is being sent to a verified security authority and not a rogue listener controlled by the attacker.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eVCSA Custom Shell Bridging:\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e Because the VCSA does not forward shell activity or denied firewall connections by default, administrators should consider implementing an agentless bridge at the Photon OS level. By configuring the \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eaudisp\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e (Audit Dispatcher) and piping \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eiptables\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e logs into the native rsyslog service, the VCSA is transformed from a passive appliance into an active sensor, capable of streaming real-time kernel-level alerts directly into the encrypted TLS pipeline.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eStandardized Retention:\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e Given this threat actor's dwell time averages 393 days, the remote syslog repository should be configured with a minimum retention period of 400 days. This allows investigators to correlate the programmatic \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eeventTypeId\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan
